Optional<T> findFirst()
Returns an Optional describing the first element of this stream, or an empty Optional if the stream is empty.

int []ab={11,13,15,17,181,19,20,21,23,24,25,26,27,28}; int num2=Arrays.stream(ab).findFirst().getAsInt(); System.out.println("Find First :: "+num2);

### Sorting Stream data

Sorting is one of the most important aspects of any programming language because it's a very common use-case that we used to implement in our application.
We can Sort collections of data either in Natural order or reverse order ,In java we have predefined functional Interfaces that enables us to sort data very easily.
![](image_rsrc3WY.jpg)We can use Comparable OR Comparator to sort collections data.
Comparable is used for Natural order Sorting whereas Comparator is used for reverse order of sorting.
We will look into multiple ways to sort collection data using default implementation for sort(..) method in Collections (its Utility class) and ArrayList.
java.util.Collections class in Java is a utility class that provides various static methods for working with collections (such as lists, sets, and maps).
It is a part of the Java Collections Framework and offers a wide range of methods for performing common operations on collections, including sorting, shuffling, searching, synchronization, and more. sort(..) method has been implemented in Collections class as below.
![](image_rsrc3WZ.jpg)Its Internally calling sort(Comparator c) form List Interface.
![](image_rsrc3X0.jpg)Lets look into Comparator interface declaration.its packaged under java.util
![](image_rsrc3X1.jpg)As we know Comparator is Functional Interface with a single abstract method compare(a,b). Lets write lambda Expression for Comparator implementation.
![](image_rsrc3X2.jpg)

Java Wrapper classes have default implementation of compare(..) method as below.i have just pasted the Integer class implementation.you can look at the other one if required.

![](image_rsrc3X3.jpg)

As you can see, the compare(..) method will return either -Ve,+Ve or 0 But what is the meaning of this return value ?

![](image_rsrc3X4.jpg)Now it's clear how compare(..) method implementation works,let's write a comparator for sorting.
![](image_rsrc3X5.jpg)We can use method reference to simplify this code, we will look into method reference later in this section.
Sort collection data using Collections class Collections.sort(numList,Integer::compareTo);

I hope we are clear with Sorting concepts,As can see that sorting and collection data can be done using Collections(Utility class) class as well as collection class like ArrayList.we will look into all possible options to sort collection data.

Lets see how we can sort Array list using Collections class:
Input: [50,30,20,10,40] Result: [10,20,30,40,50]
import java.util.ArrayList; import java.util.Collections;

public class SortingDemoArrayList {
public static void main(String[] args) {
ArrayList<Integer> numList=new ArrayList<>();
numList.add(50);
numList.add(30);
numList.add(20);
numList.add(10);
numList.add(40);

//sort(list l,Comparator i)
// here we are using comparator instance to do sorting
Collections.sort(numList,(a,b)->a.compareTo(b));
System.out.println(numList);
}
}
Here we are using Collections(List list,Comparator c) method to sort the List data.

![](image_rsrc3X6.jpg)

Input: [50,30,20,10,40] Result: [50,40,30,20,10]

import java.util.ArrayList; import java.util.Collections;

public class SortingDemoArrayList {
public static void main(String[] args) {
ArrayList<Integer> numList=new ArrayList<>(); numList.add(50);
numList.add(30);
numList.add(20);
numList.add(10);
numList.add(40);
Collections.sort(numList);
System.out.println(numList);

Collections. sort(numList,(a,b)->a.compareTo(b));
numList.sort((a,b)->-a.compareTo(b));
System.out.println(numList);

}}
In the above example we have used the Collections class to sort the ArrayList.

Let's simplify the above code : import java.util.Collections; import java.util.Comparator;

public class SortingDemoArrayList {
public static void main(String[] args) {
ArrayList<Integer> numList=new ArrayList<>(); numList.add(50);
numList.add(30);
numList.add(20);
numList.add(10);
numList.add(40);

Comparator<Integer> naturalOrderComp=(a,b)->Integer. compare(a, b); Collections.sort(numList,naturalOrderComp);
System.out.println("Natural Order :: "+numList);

Comparator<Integer> reverseOrderComp=(a,b)->Integer. compare(b, a); Collections.sort(numList,reverseOrderComp);
System.out.println("Reverse Order :: "+numList);

//Simplify the above code:
Collections.sort(numList,(a,b)->a.compareTo(b)); Collections.sort(numList,(a,b)->b.compareTo(a));

//using Method Reference to Simplify the above code Collections.sort(numList,Integer::compareTo); System.out.println("Sorted"+numList);

/*Note: You can use any of the above as per your preference*/ }
}
Result:
Natural Order :: [10, 20, 30, 40, 50]
Reverse Order :: [50, 40, 30, 20, 10]
Sorted :: [10, 20, 30, 40, 50]

We can see that ,ArrayList class also provided implementation of the sort(Comparator c) method. import java.util.Comparator;
public class SortingArrayList2 {

public static void main(String[] args) {
ArrayList<Integer> numList=new ArrayList<>(); numList.add(50);
numList.add(30);
numList.add(20);
numList.add(10);
numList.add(40);

Comparator<Integer> naturalOrderComp=(a, b)->Integer. compare(a, b); numList.sort(naturalOrderComp);
System.out.println("Natural Order : "+numList);

Comparator<Integer> reverseOrderComp=(a,b)->Integer. compare(b, a); numList.sort(reverseOrderComp);
System.out.println("Reverse Order : "+numList);

//Simplify the code
numList.sort((a, b)->Integer.compare(a, b)); System.out.println("Natural Order:2 || "+numList); numList.sort((a,b)->Integer.compare(b, a)); System.out.println("Reverse Order:2 || "+numList);

//Using Method reference to simplify the code
numList.sort(Integer::compareTo);
System.out.println("Sorting Using Method Reference :: "+numList);

}
}
Result:
Natural Order : [10, 20, 30, 40, 50]
Reverse Order : [50, 40, 30, 20, 10]
Natural Order:2 || [10, 20, 30, 40, 50]
Reverse Order:2 || [50, 40, 30, 20, 10]
Sorting Using Method Reference :: [10, 20, 30, 40, 50]

As of now we have seen that, how we can sort collection data using Collections class OR Collection class(ArrayList)
We also can use the Stream concept to sort collection data.Java Stream provides a very efficient way to sort collection data.

### Way of Sorting

Based on our needs we can opt for any approach.
sorted() :: default sorting order
sorted(Comparator c): customized sorting order.

Stream<T> sorted()
Returns a stream consisting of the elements of this stream, sorted according to natural order.
sorted() method used to sort elements in IntStream,IntLong ..types STream classes.
int []ab={11,11,25,26,4,28};
IntStream intStream=Arrays.stream(ab).sorted().distinct(); intStream.forEach(System.out::println);

Result :
4
11
25
26
28

Let's look into how to sort user-defined collection data.we have a list of Employee data and we wanted to sort Employees data based on Name

This is very common use-case in realtime application to sort data.
Suppose we are fetching Employee data from a database or we are getting this data from some external services and we need to sort all Employees in reverse order of name.

import java.util.ArrayList; import java.util.Collections; import java.util.List;

public class EmpComparableDemo {
public static void main(String[] args) {
Employee e1=new Employee("Raj","Ra@gmail.com",11l); Employee e2=new Employee("Mohit","Mohit@gmail.com",10l); Employee e3=new Employee("Abhinash","Yogit@gmail.com",14l);

List<Employee> ee= new ArrayList<>(); ee.add(e1);
ee.add(e2);
ee.add(e3);
Collections.sort(ee);
ee.forEach(System.out::println);

}
}
Result:Natural Order

Employee{name='Abhinash', email='Yogit@gmail.com', id=14} Employee{name='Mohit', email='Mohit@gmail.com', id=10} Employee{name='Raj', email='Ra@gmail.com', id=11}

Lets sort Employee collection in reverse order.

import java.util.ArrayList; import java.util.Collections; import java.util.List;

public class SortingEmployeeData {
public static void main(String[] args) {
Employee e1=new Employee("Raj","Ra@gmail.com",11l); Employee e2=new Employee("Mohit","Mohit@gmail.com",10l); Employee e3=new Employee("Abhinash","Yogit@gmail.com",14l);

List<Employee> ee= new ArrayList<>(); ee.add(e1);
ee.add(e2);
ee.add(e3);
Collections.sort(ee);

Collections. sort(ee,(a,b)->a.getName().compareTo(b.getName())); System.out.println("######## Natural Order Sorting ######"); ee.forEach(System.out::println);
Collections.sort(ee,(a,b)->-a.getName().compareTo(b.getName())); System.out.println("######## Reverse Order Sorting ######"); ee.forEach(System.out::println);

}
}

Result:
######## Natural Order Sorting ######
Employee{name='Abhinash', email='Yogit@gmail.com', id=14}

Employee{name='Mohit', email='Mohit@gmail.com', id=10} Employee{name='Raj', email='Ra@gmail.com', id=11}

######## Reverse Order Sorting ######
Employee{name='Raj', email='Ra@gmail.com', id=11} Employee{name='Mohit', email='Mohit@gmail.com', id=10} Employee{name='Abhinash', email='Yogit@gmail.com', id=14}

Later in the section we will look into a new concept , “Method Reference”. Instead of using Lambda Expression , we can use method reference to simply the code.
How does sorting work with Stream?
![](image_rsrc3X7.jpg)As we can see above, we can sort the Employee collections in Natural Order OR in reverse order by putting just simple logic.

### Sort in Reverse Order

Collections.sort(ee,(x,y)->y.getName().compareTo(x.getName())); Collections.sort(ee,(x,y)->-x.getName().compareTo(y.getName()));
As you have seen , we can sort the Employee collection in any order using Comparable and Comparator.
We will now sort the employee collection in reverse order using Comparator(I).Comparator is always recommended for customized sorting.

import java.util.ArrayList; import java.util.Collections; import java.util.List;

public class ComparableDemoWithLambda {
public static void main(String[] args) {
Comparable<Employee> c=(e1)->e1.getName().compareTo(e1.getName()); Employee e1=new Employee("Raj","Ra@gmail.com",11l); Employee e2=new Employee("Mohit","Mohit@gmail.com",10l); Employee e3=new Employee("Abhinash","Yogit@gmail.com",14l);

List<Employee> ee= new ArrayList<>();
ee.add(e1);
ee.add(e2);
ee.add(e3);
//for Reverse Order of Sorting
Collections.sort(ee,(x,y)->y.getName().compareToIgnoreCase(x.getName())); ee.forEach(System.out::println );

}
}

Result :
Employee{name='Raj', email='Ra@gmail.com', id=11}
Employee{name='Mohit', email='Mohit@gmail.com', id=10}
Employee{name='Abhinash', email='Yogit@gmail.com', id=14}

Now we will use Stream API to Sort the same Employee collection.
As we used to have huge amounts of data in a real time environment so people used to prefer Streams to Sort the collections data.
//Using Lambda Expression:
List<Employee> employeeList= ee.stream()

.sorted(Comparator.comparing(x->x.getName())) .collect(Collectors.toList());
System.out.println(employeeList);
//We have already done Sorting in section (Streams)

Result :
Employee{name='Abhinash', email='Yogit@gmail.com', id=14} Employee{name='Mohit', email='Mohit@gmail.com', id=10} Employee{name='Raj', email='Ra@gmail.com', id=11}

//using method reference

List<Employee> employeeList= ee.stream()
.sorted(Comparator.comparing(EMployee::getName())) .collect(Collectors.toList());

System.out.println(employeeList);
//We have already done Sorting in section (Streams)

Result :
Employee{name='Abhinash', email='Yogit@gmail.com', id=14} Employee{name='Mohit', email='Mohit@gmail.com', id=10} Employee{name='Raj', email='Ra@gmail.com', id=11}

As we can see above two examples,we are using Comparator to sort the data.Stream provides a set of methods that help to sort the collection data very easily and reliably.

Comparator(I)
Functional interface basically used to sort the collection data and streams element. Ordering can be Natural(ascending) OR Reversed (Descending) order.

public abstract int compare(T, T); public abstract boolean equals(Object);
Below 3 methods dont take any args:

public default Comparator<T> reversed();
public static <T extends Comparable<? super T>> Comparator<T> reverseOrder(); public static <T extends Comparable<? super T>> Comparator<T> naturalOrder();

We used these methods for chaining the sorting process, you want to sort your Object based on multiple attributes.
public default Comparator<T> thenComparing(Comparator<? super T>); public default <U> Comparator<T> thenComparing(Function<? super T, ? extends U>,

Comparator<? super U>);
public default <U extends Comparable<? super U>> Comparator<T>
thenComparing(Function<? super T,? extends U>);
public default Comparator<T> thenComparingInt(ToIntFunction<? super T>); public default Comparator<T> thenComparingLong(ToLongFunction<? super T>); public default .Comparator<T> thenComparingDouble(ToDoubleFunction<? super
T>);

Moving NULL data to last or First on collection/Streams
public static <T> Comparator<T> nullsFirst(Comparator<? super T>); public static <T> Comparator<T> nullsLast(Comparator<? super T>);

public static <T, U extends Comparable<? super U>> Comparator<T> comparing(Function<? super T, ? extends U>);
public static <T, U> Comparator<T> comparing(Function<? super T, ? extends U>, Comparator<? super U>);

Applicable for ToIntFunction,ToDoubleFunction and ToDoubleFunction .functional interfaces
we used these to sort custom objects like(our used defined class object like Employee,Student)

public static <T> Comparator<T> comparingInt(ToIntFunction<? super T>); public static <T> Comparator<T> comparingLong(ToLongFunction<? super T>); public static <T> Comparator<T> comparingDouble(ToDoubleFunction<? super T>);

Stream<T> sorted(Comparator<? super T> comparator)
Returns a stream consisting of the elements of this stream, sorted according to the provided Comparator.

Integer []ac={ 22,229,44,99,55,11,110};
List<Integer> numList1=Arrays.stream(ac).sorted(Comparator.naturalOrder()) .collect(Collectors.toList());
List<Integer> numList2=Arrays.stream(ac).sorted(Comparator.reverseOrder()) .collect(Collectors.toList());

System.out.println(numList1); System.out.println(numList2);

Result :
[11, 22, 44, 55, 99, 110, 229] [229, 110, 99, 55, 44, 22, 11]

Simple way to sorting Streams:

Integer []ac={ 22,44,99,55,11,110}; //natural order Arrays.stream(ac).sorted((t,u)->t.compareTo(u)).forEach(System.out::println);

//descending order
Arrays.stream(ac).sorted((t,u)->-t.compareTo(u)).forEach(System.out::println); Arrays.stream(ac).sorted((t,u)->u.compareTo(t)).forEach(System.out::println);

Result :
11 22 44 55 99 110
110 99 55 44 22 11
110 99 55 44 22 11

Sorting custom class object
Let's create some uti class that will provide an Employee list.in realtime we used to get these data from databases or some other stores.
import java8.EmployeeBean;
import java.util.List;
public class EmployeeUtil {
public static List<EmployeeBean> getAllEmployeeList(){

EmployeeBean e1=  new EmployeeBean("Raj","Patna","Development","SSE",140000);
EmployeeBean e2=new EmployeeBean("Mohan","Bangalore","Development","SSE",160000); EmployeeBean e3=new EmployeeBean("Amit","Delhi","Development","SSE",110000);
EmployeeBean e4=new EmployeeBean("Heera","Patna","R&D","SSE",240000);
EmployeeBean e5=new EmployeeBean("Jawed","Bangalore","Development","SSE",140000); EmployeeBean e6=new EmployeeBean("Khali","Delhi","R&D","SSE",340000);
EmployeeBean e7=new EmployeeBean("Abhinash","Bangalore","Development","SSE",140000); List<EmployeeBean> list =List.of(e1,e2,e3,e4,e5,e6,e7);  return list;

}
}
Example 2: Using Lambda Expression
import java8.EmployeeBean;

import java.util.Comparator; import java.util.List;
import java.util.stream.Collectors;

public class SortEmployeeWithStream2 {  public static void main(String[] args) {

List<EmployeeBean> employeeList =EmployeeUtil. getAllEmployeeList(); System.out.println("Before Sorting ::");
employeeList.forEach(x->System.out.println(x));

List<EmployeeBean> sortedEMpDataNaturalOrder=employeeList.stream() .sorted(Comparator.comparing(e->e.getName()))
.collect(Collectors.toList());

System.out.println("After Sorting :: Natural Order ::>");
sortedEMpDataNaturalOrder.forEach(x->System.out.println(x));
List<EmployeeBean> sortedEMpDataReverseOrder=employeeList.stream() .sorted((a,b)->a.getName().compareTo(b.getName()))
.collect(Collectors.toList());
System.out.println("After Sorting :: Reverse Order ::>"); sortedEMpDataNaturalOrder.forEach(x->System.out.println(x)); }
}

As we have done sorting using Lambda Expression As well Method reference. We have done sorting by given attribute. You can try with multiple attributes.
sort Employee by Name and CITY.  import java8.EmployeeBean;

import java.util.Comparator; import java.util.List;
import java.util.stream.Collectors;

public class SortEmployeeByMultipleField { public static void main(String[] args) {

List<EmployeeBean> employeeList =EmployeeUtil. getAllEmployeeList(); System.out.println("Before Sorting ::");
employeeList.forEach(System.out::println);

List<EmployeeBean> sortedEMpDataNaturalOrder=employeeList.stream() .sorted(Comparator.comparing(EmployeeBean::getName)) .collect(Collectors.toList());

System.out.println("After Sorting :: Natural Order ::>");
sortedEMpDataNaturalOrder.forEach(System.out::println);
List<EmployeeBean> sortByNameAndCity=employeeList.stream() .sorted(Comparator.comparing(EmployeeBean::getName)

.thenComparing(EmployeeBean::getCity))
.collect(Collectors.toList());
System.out.println("After Sorting :: By Name & City ::>"); sortByNameAndCity.forEach(System.out::println);

List<EmployeeBean> sortByNameAndCityReverse=employeeList.stream() .sorted(Comparator.comparing(EmployeeBean::getName)

.thenComparing(EmployeeBean::getCity).reversed())
.collect(Collectors.toList());
System.out.println("After Sorting ::By Name & City :: Reverse Order ::>"); sortByNameAndCityReverse.forEach(System.out::println);

}
}

Before Sorting ::

{name='Jawed', city='Bangalore', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=140000} {name='Jawed', city='Delhi', dept='R&D', desig='SSE', salary=340000}
{name='Abhinash', city='Bangalore', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=140000}
