Sort Employee in reversed order by Employee Salary:  import java8.EmployeeBean;

import java.util.Comparator; import java.util.List;
import java.util.stream.Collectors;

public class SortEmployeeBySalary {
public static void main(String[] args) {
List<EmployeeBean> employeeList =EmployeeUtil.getAllEmployeeList(); System.out.println("############## Before Sorting ##########"); employeeList.forEach(System.out::println);
List<EmployeeBean> employeeBeansObj= employeeList.stream()

.sorted(Comparator. comparingDouble(EmployeeBean::getSalary).reversed()) .collect(Collectors.toList());
System.out.println("### After Sorting:: reverse Order by Salary ###"); employeeBeansObj.forEach(System.out::println);
}
}

############## Before Sorting ##########
{name='Raj', city='Patna', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=140000} {name='Mohan', city='Bangalore', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=160000} {name='Amit', city='Delhi', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=110000} {name='Heera', city='Patna', dept='R&D', desig='SSE', salary=240000} {name='Jawed', city='Bangalore', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=140000} {name='Khali', city='Delhi', dept='R&D', desig='SSE', salary=340000} {name='Abhinash', city='Bangalore', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=140000}

### After Sorting:: reverse Order by Salary ###
{name='Khali', city='Delhi', dept='R&D', desig='SSE', salary=340000} {name='Heera', city='Patna', dept='R&D', desig='SSE', salary=240000} {name='Mohan', city='Bangalore', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=160000} {name='Raj', city='Patna', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=140000} {name='Jawed', city='Bangalore', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=140000} {name='Abhinash', city='Bangalore', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=140000} {name='Amit', city='Delhi', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=110000}

In the above example we sort Employee data based on name and salary in reversed order. So this reverse ordering applied on both fields.

What if I want a separate comparator to sort Employee data by Name in Natural order and Salary in reverse order.
Here we need to use a different comparator.

List<EmployeeBean> empList=AppUtil.getEmpList(); List<EmployeeBean> empByNameAndSalary=empList.stream()
.sorted(Comparator.comparingDouble(EmployeeBean::getSalary).reversed()) .sorted(Comparator.comparing(EmployeeBean::getName))
.collect(Collectors.toList());
empByNameAndSalary.forEach(x->System.out.println("Name : "+x.getName()+", Salary : "+x.getSalary()));
Result:

Name : Amir, Salary : 170000.0 Name : Amir, Salary : 80000.0 Name : Dharam, Salary : 90000.0 Name : Krish, Salary : 100000.0 Name : Marina, Salary : 110000.0 Name : Mohan, Salary : 60000.0 Name : Raj, Salary : 50000.0 Name : Salman, Salary : 70000.0 Name : Saloni, Salary : 160000.0 Name : Saloni, Salary : 140000.0 Name : Saloni, Salary : 120000.0

Based on your need you need to see what kind of sorting you need ,based on that you need to decide whether you need a separate Comparator or NOT.remember that using separate comparator will consume more system resources so perf will be impacted.

Skipping Stream Element

Stream<T> skip(long n)
Returns a stream consisting of the remaining elements of this stream after discarding the first n elements of the stream.

Integer []ac={ 22,44,99,55,11,110};
Arrays.stream(ac).skip(2).forEach(System.out::println); Result : 99 55 11 110

Find Min & Max from Stream

min(Comparator<? super T> comparator) max(Comparator<? super T> comparator) Find MIN and MAX value

These Methods take arg as Comparator.
Find MAX and MIN from Integer Array

Integer []ac={ 22,229,44,99,55,11,110};
int max=Arrays.stream(ac).mapToInt(x->x).max().getAsInt(); int min=Arrays.stream(ac).mapToInt(x->x).min().getAsInt(); System.out.println("MAX : "+max);
System.out.println("MIN : "+min); Result:

MAX : 229
MIN : 11  Find max value from int array

int element[]={22,229,44,99,55,11,110};
int minVal=Arrays.stream(element).min().getAsInt(); int maxVal=Arrays.stream(element).max().getAsInt(); System.out.println("MAX : "+maxVal);
System.out.println("MIN : "+minVal); Result:

MAX : 229
MIN : 11
Use-case: let's find the highest salary Employee. Find the Employee with MAX salary in Organization: List<EmployeeBean> empList=AppUtil.getEmpList();
EmployeeBean employeeBean=empList.stream()
.max(Comparator.comparing(EmployeeBean::getSalary)).get();
System.out.println(employeeBean); Result:
EmployeeBean{name='Amir', city='Bangalore', dept='Gov', desig='Arch', salary=170000.0}
Find MAX salary in Organization

OptionalDouble salary=empList.stream() .mapToDouble(EmployeeBean::getSalary) .max();

System.out.println(salary.getAsDouble()); Result: 170000.0
Find the Employee with MIN salary in Organization
List<EmployeeBean> empList=AppUtil.getEmpList();
EmployeeBean employeeBean=empList.stream()
.min(Comparator.comparing(EmployeeBean::getSalary)).get();
System.out.println(employeeBean); Result: EmployeeBean{name='Raj', city='Patna', dept='R&D', desig='SSE', salary=50000.0}
Find MIN salary in Organization

OptionalDouble salary=empList.stream() .mapToDouble(EmployeeBean::getSalary) .min();

System.out.println(salary.getAsDouble());
Result : 50000.0
Flattening Stream data
<R> Stream<R> flatMap(Function<? super T,? extends Stream<? extends R>> mapper)  It used to flatten the collections data.
What is flatmap(..)

The flatMap operation in Java streams is used to flatten nested collections or to transform and concatenate elements in a stream of collections into a single stream. It's commonly used when you have a stream of elements, each of which contains another collection, and you want to operate on the elements within those nested collections.

flatMap() = Flattening (flat)+ mapping (map)
![](image_rsrc3X8.jpg)Just Imagine we have a bunch of boxes, and each box contains some items. Now, we want to take out all those items from the boxes and put them in a single box. That’s what flatMap() does with a stream.
List<Integer> num1=List.of(9,2,3);
List<Integer> num2=List.of(4,5,6);
List<List<Integer>> lists=List.of(num1,num2);
System.out.println(lists);
List<Integer> finalList=lists.stream().flatMap(List::stream).collect(Collectors.toList()); System.out.println(finalList);

Result:
Input List: [[9, 2, 3], [4, 5, 6]]
Output List: [2, 3, 9, 4, 5, 6]

How does data Flattening work with Stream ?
![](image_rsrc3X9.jpg)It simply creates the Stream of each element in this list and then Flatten it and merge in a single Stream.

flatten the 2d array to a list of String
Input : {{"Raj","Mahi"},{"Bikash","Rana"},{"Abhinash","Rajiv"}} Output: [Raj, Mahi, Bikash, Rana, Abhinash, Rajiv]

String names[][]={{"Raj","Mahi"},{"Bikash","Rana"},{"Abhinash","Rajiv"}};

List<String> nameList= Arrays. stream(names)
.flatMap(x-> Arrays.stream(x))
.collect(Collectors.toList());

System.out.println(nameList); Result: [Raj, Mahi, Bikash, Rana, Abhinash, Rajiv]
Primitive version of data flattening

IntStream flatMapToInt(Function<? super T,? extends IntStream> mapper) LongStream flatMapToLong(Function<? super T,? extends LongStream> mapper) DoubleStream flatMapToDouble(Function<? super T,? extends DoubleStream> mapper)

List<Integer> num1=Arrays. asList(1, 2, 3);
List<Integer> num2=Arrays.asList(4, 5);
List<Integer> num3=Arrays.asList(6, 7, 8);
List<List<Integer>> rr=List.of(num1,num2,num3);
rr.stream().flatMapToInt(x->x.stream().mapToInt(Integer::intValue)).forEach(System.out::println);

Result :
1 2 3 4 5 6 7 8

## Important Java features : Java 8+

### Default method in java

Before moving forward,let me assure you that you are using Java 8+.
Default method concept was introduced as part of java 8.

Before java 8,interfaces could have only abstract methods but as part of java 8 release private methods(java 9) and default methods(java 8) can be part of interfaces.

Lets understand why default method was introduced as part of java,as i asked that before java 8 only abstract method can be part of an interface and adding any additional abstract method to any existing interface was breaking backward compatibility because clients need to surely implement all the abstract method of interface they are consuming.

To solve the backward compatibility problem ,a default method was introduced.default method will be the same as concrete method the only difference is that it will have access modifier “default”.

Default method will have some default implementation so that clients don't need to implement this method if they don't need to.based on their requirements they can override the behavior of this method.

default int addTwoNumbers(){ //some default implementation return 0;
}

Below are some common benefits of default method
● Backward Compatibility

One of the primary reasons for introducing default methods was to allow interfaces to evolve without breaking existing classes that implement those interfaces. In other words, default methods provide a way to add new methods to an interface without forcing all implementing classes to provide implementations for those new methods. This is crucial for libraries and APIs that need to be extended over time while still maintaining compatibility with existing code.

● Support for Lambda Expressions

Java 8 introduced lambda expressions and the Stream API, which rely heavily on interfaces with a single abstract method (SAM interfaces), also known as functional interfaces. To make it easier to work with functional interfaces and provide additional utility methods, default methods were introduced. This allowed existing interfaces like java.util.List to add new methods like forEach without breaking compatibility.

● Code Reuse and Utility Methods

Default methods can also include utility methods that are shared among multiple classes that implement the same interface. This promotes code reuse and reduces code duplication in implementing classes. For example, the Collection interface in Java 8 introduced several useful default methods, such as stream(), forEach(), and removeIf(), which can be used by any class implementing the Collection interface.

● Gradual Migration

Default methods provide a path for gradually migrating legacy code to newer versions of an interface. Existing classes can choose to override and provide their own implementations for

default methods, or they can rely on the default implementation provided by the interface. This gives developers flexibility in how they upgrade their codebases.
Let's consider the use-case,We have one interface CalulatorService where we have one method : addTwoNumbers(int ,int) and we have multiple clients that implement this interface.

public interface CalculatorService { public int addTwoNumbers(int a,int b);
}
public class CalculatorServiceImpl implements CalculatorService{ @Override
public int addTwoNumbers(int a, int b) {
return (a+b);
}
}

//there may be multiples class that implement this interface
public class CalculatorServiceDemo {
public static void main(String[] args) {
CalculatorServiceImpl service=new CalculatorServiceImpl();
int sum=service.addTwoNumbers(10,20); System.out.println("SUM :: "+sum); }
}
Result:
SUM :: 30
As we know above code will work as expected.
Now we have some requirements and we just wanted to introduce some additional methods to that interface.

If i will add any additional method to that existing interface then all the clients need to implement that method else they will get a compile time error.it may be possible that some of our clients don't want to use this newly introduced method so it will surely get a compile time error.

Even if they want to implement this method, they need to refactor the existing implementation if they are using method reference because this interface is no longer a functional interface because we have multiple abstract methods and its very hard to refactor such existing code in real time because we need to check and validate all the dependent services who is consuming this interface and its functions.

So to overcome such a situation ,a default method was introduced in java 8.

If you will be looking into existing java classes like List,Set,Map and some others you will get that some new methods were introduced in these existing interfaces to make java more flexible and reliable.
Let's introduce one more method multiplyTwoNumbers (int ,int) to existing interface CalculatorService, it will throw a compile time error and the existing implementation will also be broken if they are using method reference.

public interface CalculatorService { public int addTwoNumbers(int a,int b);
public int multiplyTwoNumbers(int a,int b); }
Lets see how we can resolve the problem:
public interface CalculatorService { public int addTwoNumbers(int a,int b);
//access modifier is ‘default’
default int multiplyTwoNumbers(int a,int b){ return a+b;
}
//you can add any no of default method here }
As you can see here,access modifier is “default” and there is default body given for this method.this method looks like a concrete method that we used to have in java classes.
So now from java 8+ onwards , we can declare default methods in the interfaces. Here, the client can override the behavior of this default method as per their requirements and this change will be backward compatible.
Syntax of default method

default returnType methodName(..){ //Method body
}

import lombok.ToString;
@ToString
public class StudentDemo {

String name;
public StudentDemo() {
}
public StudentDemo(String name) {

this .name = name;
}
void printWelcome(){

System.out.println("Welcome to Student Class"); }
}

### Private Method in Interface

● private method was introduced in java 9
● Interfaces can have private methods as well as static methods.
● Private method will be useful to hide method implementation from the client.you can introduce

private methods inside the interface for your internal usage and it will NOT be accessible to any one since it's private by nature.
● Private method used to create confidential operations.
Same like that you can declare static methods also.

import functionalJava.bean.Student; public interface StudentService {
abstract Student getStudentData();
default int getStudentPrivateData(int sid){ int pass= getStudentAccPass(sid); //method body
return pass+12345;
}
private int getStudentAccPass(int sid){ //method body
return 123;
}}

### Local Variable Type Inference

It was introduced in Java 10.
Local Variable Type Inference (often referred to as "var") allows you to declare local variables without explicitly specifying their data types.
Compiler will get type from initialization.
var name=new String(“raj”);
As we can see in the above example, The type of ”name” is taken from the right side expression.compiler will automatically take care of it.
Before Local Method Inference
ArrayList<String> names = new ArrayList<>();
After Local Method Inference

var names = new ArrayList<String>(); var name=”raj”;
import functionalJava.bean.EmployeeBean; import util.EmployeeUtil;

import java.util.stream.Collectors;
public class LocalMethodInferenceDemo { public static void main(String[] args) {
var employeeBeanList =EmployeeUtil.getAllEmployeeList(); System.out.println("#### employeeBeanList :: "+employeeBeanList);

var filteredEmpList=employeeBeanList.stream()
.map(EmployeeBean::getName)
.collect(Collectors.toList());

System.out.println("## filteredEmpList ## "+filteredEmpList);

var groupByName=employeeBeanList.stream()
.collect(Collectors.groupingBy(EmployeeBean::getName));
System.out.println("## groupByName :: "+groupByName);

var partitionData=employeeBeanList.stream()
.collect(Collectors.partitioningBy(x->x.getName().startsWith("A")));
System.out.println("### partitionData :: "+partitionData); }}

Result :
#### employeeBeanList :: [{name='Raj', city='Patna', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=140000}, {name='Raj', city='Bangalore', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=160000}, {name='Amit', city='Delhi', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=110000}, {name='Jawed', city='Patna', dept='R&D', desig='SSE', salary=240000}, {name='Jawed', city='Bangalore', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=140000}, {name='Jawed', city='Delhi', dept='R&D', desig='SSE', salary=340000}, {name='Abhinash', city='Bangalore', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=140000}]
## filteredEmpList ## [Raj, Raj, Amit, Jawed, Jawed, Jawed, Abhinash]
## groupByName :: {Abhinash=[{name='Abhinash', city='Bangalore', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=140000}], Amit=[{name='Amit', city='Delhi', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=110000}], Raj=[{name='Raj', city='Patna', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=140000}, {name='Raj', city='Bangalore', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=160000}], Jawed=[{name='Jawed', city='Patna', dept='R&D', desig='SSE', salary=240000}, {name='Jawed', city='Bangalore', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=140000}, {name='Jawed', city='Delhi', dept='R&D', desig='SSE', salary=340000}]}
### partitionData :: {false=[{name='Raj', city='Patna', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=140000}, {name='Raj', city='Bangalore', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=160000}, {name='Jawed', city='Patna', dept='R&D', desig='SSE', salary=240000}, {name='Jawed', city='Bangalore', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=140000}, {name='Jawed', city='Delhi', dept='R&D', desig='SSE', salary=340000}], true=[{name='Amit', city='Delhi', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=110000}, {name='Abhinash', city='Bangalore', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=140000}]}

## Optional in Java 8

### ➔ Optional class introduced as part of java.util package in java 8\. ➔ This is mostly used to simplify the NULL handling for any object in java.As we know NullPointerException is one the most common exception that we used to get if any operation performed on Null value.
➔ If we are handling with Stream,String or any other java object ,it may be possible that data is NULL and we need to put Null check all the places which is a tedious job.solution for this problem was introduced as Optional class in java as part of java 8 release.
So it's very simple to handle Null values now using Optional.
Container objects may or may NOT contain Null values.Optional class has a very handy method isPresent() that will return boolean value.
isPresent(): TRUE ,mean container is not Null Is Present() : FALSE : means container Object is Null
Methods in Optional class Below are a set of methods available in Optional class.

![](image_rsrc3XA.jpg)Lets see Optional Method one by one:

### of(T value)

Creates an Optional with a non-null value.
Create Option from String:
Optional<String> optional = Optional.of("Raj");

Create Option from Custom class Object:
List<EmployeeBean> employeeBeans =EmployeeUtil.getAllEmployeeList(); Optional<List<EmployeeBean>> data=Optional.of(employeeBeans);

Optional<String> optional1=Option.of(null);
boolean isPresent2 = optional1.isPresent(); // NullPointerException

### empty()

Creates an empty Optional.
Optional<String> emptyOptionalData = Optional.empty();

### ofNullable(T value)

Creates an Optional with a value, which can be null. If the value is null, then an empty Optional object is returned

String addr = null;
Optional<String> optional = Optional.ofNullable(addr); boolean isPresent2 = optional.isPresent(); // true

### isPresent()

Checks if a value is present in the Optional. It will just check value is present there or not Optional<String> optional = Optional.of("Raj"); boolean isPresent = optional.isPresent(); // true

Optional<String> optional1 = Optional.of(null);
boolean isPresent1 = optional1.isPresent(); //NullPointerException
Optional<String> optional2 = Optional.ofNullable(null); boolean isPresent2 = optional2.isPresent(); // true

### ifPresent(Consumer<T> consumer)

Executes the given consumer function if a value is present.

Optional<String> optional = Optional.of("Raj");
optional.ifPresent(value -> System.out.println("Value: " + value)); Result : Raj

Optional<String> optional3 = Optional.of("Raj");
optional3.ifPresent(value -> System.out.println("Value: " + value));
Optional<String> optional4 = Optional.of(null);
optional4.ifPresent(value -> System.out.println("Value: " + value));

Result:
false
Value: Raj

Optional<String> optional5 = Optional.ofNullable(null); optional5.ifPresent(value -> System.out.println("Value: " + value));

Result: It will Throw NullPointerException Optional<EmployeeBean> optional6 = Optional.ofNullable(new EmployeeBean("Raj","Patna","Development","SSE",140000)); optional6.ifPresent(value -> {

if(value.getSalary()>50000){
System.out.println(" Emp :"+value);
}
});

Optional<List<EmployeeBean>> optional7 =
Optional.ofNullable(EmployeeUtil.getAllEmployeeList()); System.out.println("Input Data :: "+optional7.get()); optional7.ifPresent(value -> {
System.out.println("######### filtered data :");

value.stream().filter(x->x.getSalary()>200000).collect(Collectors.toList()) .forEach(System.out::println);
});
Result:
Emp :{name='Raj', city='Patna', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=140000}
Input Data ::

[{name='Raj', city='Patna', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=140000}, {name='Raj', city='Bangalore', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=160000}, {name='Amit', city='Delhi', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=110000}, {name='Jawed', city='Patna', dept='R&D', desig='SSE', salary=240000}, {name='Jawed', city='Bangalore', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=140000},

{name='Jawed', city='Delhi', dept='R&D', desig='SSE', salary=340000}, {name='Abhinash', city='Bangalore', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=140000}]

######### filtered data :
{name='Jawed', city='Patna', dept='R&D', desig='SSE', salary=240000} {name='Jawed', city='Delhi', dept='R&D', desig='SSE', salary=340000}

### orElse(T other)

Returns the value if present; otherwise, returns the provided default value.
We used to have some use-cases where we return some default value if NO value is present in Optional String result = optional8.orElse("Raj"); System.out.println(result);

List<EmployeeBean> ll= new ArrayList<>();
EmployeeBean employeeBean1=new
EmployeeBean("Raj","Patna","Development","SSE",140000);

List<EmployeeBean> defaultEmpList= new ArrayList<>(); defaultEmpList.add(employeeBean1);
Optional.ofNullable(ll)

.orElse(defaultEmpList);
Result: Raj
System.out.println( Optional.empty() .orElse(defaultEmpList));
Result:
[{name='Raj', city='Patna', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=140000}]

### orElseGet(Supplier<T> supplier)

It's helpful to provide some default value from the supplier if Optional is empty OR No value present. You can use any existing Supplier to provide default data.
This is similar to orElse(T other), it just has a supplier as input whereas orElseGet(Supplier<T> supplier) takes any type T

Supplier<EmployeeBean> getEmpData=()-> new
EmployeeBean("Raj","Patna","Development","SSE",140000); Object employeeBean2=Optional.empty().orElseGet(getEmpData); System.out.println(employeeBean2);

Result:
{name='Raj', city='Patna', dept='Development', desig='SSE', salary=140000}

### orElseThrow(Supplier<? extends X> exceptionSupplier)

Returns the value if present; otherwise, throws an exception generated by the provided supplier.
Again it's for handling null data, if NO data then throw some exception. String result1 = optional9.orElseThrow(() -> new NullPointerException("No Data Found"));
Result:

It will throw below Exception.
Exception in thread "main" java.util.NoSuchElementException: No value present at java.base/java.util.Optional.orElseThrow(Optional.java:377) at
functionalJava.optionalDemo.OptionalClassDemo.main(OptionalClassDemo.java:87)

get()
Returns the value if present; otherwise, throws a NoSuchElementException.
It's just used to get data from Option instances.

Optional<String> getVal=Optional. of("Raj"); String name=getVal.get();
System.out.println(name);

Result: Raj

### filter(Predicate<T> predicate)

Applies a filter to the value if present. If the value matches the predicate, it's retained; otherwise, an empty Optional is returned.

Optional<Integer> optional10 = Optional. of(123);
Optional<Integer> filtered = optional10.filter(value -> value > 100); System.out.println("## filtered "+filtered.get());

Result: 123 Optional<Integer> optional11 = Optional.empty();
Optional<Integer> filtered1 = optional11.filter(value -> value > 100); System.out.println("## filtered1 "+filtered1.get());

Result:
It will Throw Exception

Exception in thread "main" java.util.NoSuchElementException: No value present at java.base/java.util.Optional.get(Optional.java:143)
at

functionalJava.optionalDemo.OptionalClassDemo.main(OptionalClassDemo.java:105)
If No value Satisfied the predicate THEN Optional will be Empty

Optional<Integer> optional10 = Optional. of(123);
Optional<Integer> filtered = optional10.filter(value -> value > 888); System.out.println("## filtered "+filtered.get());

Result:
Exception in thread "main" java.util.NoSuchElementException: No value present at java.base/java.util.Optional.get(Optional.java:143)
at
functionalJava.optionalDemo.OptionalClassDemo.main(OptionalClassDemo.java:101)

### map(Function<? super T,? extends U> mapper)
It used to map the value in Optional. if value present THEN returns a new Optional containing the result.
If Optional is empty THEN it will throw Exception

Optional<String> optional12 = Optional. of("Raj");
Optional<Integer> length = optional.map(value -> value.length()); System.out.println(length.get());

Result:3

String empName= Optional. of(new
EmployeeBean("Raj","Patna","Development","SSE",140000)) .map(x->x.getName())
.get();

System.out.println("## empName "+empName);
Result: Raj

### flatMap(Function<? super T,Optional<U>> mapper)

flatMap is similar to map with additional feature flattering. It will map data with flattening.
As we have seen map() and flatMap() of Stream.this also have the same behavior and it can be applied on Optional Objects directly.
Optional<Optional<String>> data12=Optional.of(Optional.of("Raj"));
data12.map(x->x).ifPresent(System.out::println);
data12.flatMap(x->x).ifPresent(System.out::println);
Result:
Optional[Raj] Raj

### java switch statement: from java 7 to java 20

➔ Switch statements can be used instead of using multiple if-else statements.
➔ switch statement executes one statement from multiple conditions so how it helps us to avoid an if-else ladder in code.
There is so much enhancement on switch statements in java from java 7 to java 20. We will have sample code for switch statement from java 7 to java 20  Switch statement : java 7
Till java 7 only int was accepted as part of switch case argument.  public static void java7SwitchDemo(int num){
switch (num){
case 1:
System.out.println("sss is case 1");
break;
case 2:
System.out.println("this is case 2");
break;
case 3:
System.out.println("this is case 3");
break;
default:
System.out.println("this is default case");
}
}
Java 8 : in java 8 String and Enum are allowed as switch case args  public static void java8SwitchDemoString(String num){
switch (num){
case "Monday":
System.out.println("Its Monday today");
break;
case "Tuesday":
System.out.println("Its Tuesday today");
break;
case "Wed":
System.out.println("Its Wed today");
break;
default:
System.out.println("Please provide the valid day of the week"); }
}
Switch with Enum:with Break statement  public static void java8SwitchDemoEnum(Days num){
switch (num){
case MONDAY:
System.out.println("Its Monday today");
break;
case TUESDAY:

System. out.println("Its Tuesday today");
break;
case WED:

System.out.println("Its Wed today");
break;
default:
System.out.println("Please provide the valid day of the week"); }
}
Switch with Return:  public static String java12SwitchDemoReturn(Days num){ switch (num){
case MONDAY:

return  "Its Monday today";
case TUESDAY:
return "Its Tuesday today";
case WED:
return "Its Wed today";
default:
return "Please provide the valid day of the week"; }
}

public static void java12SwitchDemoArrow(Days num){
switch (num){
case MONDAY -> System.out.println("This is Monday");
case TUESDAY-> System.out.println("Its Tuesday today"); case WED-> System.out.println("Its Wed today");
default-> System.out.println("Please provide the valid day of the week");

}
}
Switch with Arrow:  public static void javaSwitchWithArrow(Days num){

String dayOfTheWeek= switch (num) {
case MONDAY -> "Monday";
case TUESDAY -> "Tuesday";
case WED -> "Wed";
default -> "invalid";

};
System.out.println(dayOfTheWeek);
}
Switch with Multi case : switch case as expression  public static void switchWithMultiCase(Days num){

String dayOfTheWeek= switch (num) {
case MONDAY,TUESDAY,WED,THURS,FRI -> "WeekDays"; case SAT ,SUNDAY-> "Weekend";
default -> "invalid";

};
System.out.println(dayOfTheWeek);
}
Switch with NULL case: switch case as expression

public static String SwitchWithNullCase(Object bar) { return switch(bar) {
case Integer i -> "I'm an Integer: " + i; case Long l -> "I'm a Long: " + l;
case null ->"This is NUll";
default -> "I'm an object";

};
}
Switch with Pattern match:

public static void switchWIthGuardedPattern(Object bar) { switch(bar) {
case Integer i -> System.out.println("This is Integer"); case EmployeeTest l -> System.out.println("This is emp."); case EmpRecord e -> System.out.println("This is Record."); default -> System.out.println("This is default.");

}
}
public static String switchWIthGuardedPatternWithReturn(Object bar) { return switch(bar) {

case Integer i -> "I'm an Integer: " + i;
case EmployeeTest l -> "I'm a Emp: " + l.eid;
case EmpRecord e ->"I am record"+e.email();
default -> "I'm an object";

};
}
public static String switchWIthGuardedPatternWithReturn2(Object bar) { switch(bar) {
case Integer i -> {
return "Integer";
}
case EmployeeTest l -> {
return "Employee";
}
case EmpRecord e -> {
return "EmpRecord";
}
default -> {
return "Default";
}
}
}
Switch with Guarded Pattern:java 20  public static String switchWIthGuardedPatternWithReturn3(Object bar) { switch(bar) {
case Integer i && i<200-> {

return  "Integer";
}
case EmployeeTest l && l.eid>10 -> {

return  "Employee";
}
case EmpRecord e &&e.eid()>2-> {

return  "EmpRecord";
}
default -> {

return "Default";
}
}
}

## Record class: java 17 feature

### Record is a special class in java.its mostly used for data carrier classes/DTO in java.it was introduced as part of java 14. In any project, it is a common use-case to convert data from DTO to entity and viceversa. For data DTO classes we used to create: 1\. Setter method 2\. Getter method 3\. constructor 4\. toString method 5\. Hashcode method 6\. Equals method
Although we have some plugins to take care of such things in java, if those plugins are not getting used in your project then you have to create/generate all these methods and the class will become too huge.
You can use the lombok library to make it simple but java has no such feature built to take care of it by default before java 14.
Now we will see the DTO/Data carrier class with and without Record classes.
The only difference is that if you are using Records classes instead of normal Java classes then you no need to create your DTO classes with getter/setter and other methods as we saw above.
Record class demo

Sample DTO:  package playwithjava.in.testt;  import java.util.Objects;  public class EmployeeDto {

private String name;
private String email;
private Long empId;
public EmployeeDto(String name, String email, Long empId) {

this .name = name;
this.email = email;
this.empId = empId;

}
public EmployeeDto(String name, String email) {
this.name = name;
this.email = email;
}

public String getName() { return name;
}

public void setName(String name) { this.name = name;
}

public String getEmail() { return email;
}

public void setEmail(String email) { this.email = email;
}

public Long getEmpId() { return empId;
}

public void setEmpId(Long empId) { this.empId = empId;
}
return name.equals(that.name) && email.equals(that.email) && empId.equals(that.empId);
}

@Override
public boolean equals(Object o) {
if (this == o) return true;
if (o == null || getClass() != o.getClass()) return false;
EmployeeDto that = (EmployeeDto) o;

@Override  public int hashCode() {  return Objects.hash(name, email, empId); }
}  Lets use lombok plugin to simplify it:

import lombok.*;
@Data
@NoArgsConstructor
@AllArgsConstructor
@ToString
@EqualsAndHashCode public class EmployeeDto {

private String name; private String email; private Long empId;

}
Use record  record EmployeeDto(String name,String email,Long empId){ }

### 1\. It doesn't have a no-arg constructor. 2\. record is immutable by default so it's final and it cant be extended. 3\. It will create a public constructor with each field.

public EmployeeDto(String name, String email, Long empId) { this.name = name;
this.email = email;
this.empId = empId;

}
Only this constructor will be available for this record(3 fields).if you want a constructor with 2 fields or one field or no-arg constructor you need to create it explicitly.

EmployeeDto employeeDto= new EmployeeDto("Raj","raj@gmail.com",122l); EmployeeDto employeeDto=new EmployeeDto("Raj","raj@gmail.com"); // this will throw a compile time error.

You need to create 2 args constructors for this explicitly. EmployeeDto employeeDto=new EmployeeDto();  You need to create a no-arg constructor for this explicitly.

### Java will create getter,setter,toString,equals,hashCode and parameterized constructors with all class attributes(ONLY all param constructors) will automatically be created.
You can also create static variable in the records:

record EmployeeDto(String name,String email,Long empId) { public static String city = "Bangalore";
}

static method can also be created:  record EmployeeDto(String name,String email,Long empId) { public static String getName(String name) {
return name;
}
}
Static methods and variables can be accessed the same as java classes.

record EmployeeDto(String name,String email,Long empId) { public static final String CITY="Bangalore";
public static String getName(String name) {

return name;
}
}
public class RecordTester {
public static void main(String[] args) {

var name=EmployeeDto.getName("Raj"); var cityVal=EmployeeDto.CITY; System.out.println(name);
System.out.println(cityVal);

}}

## Sealed Classes in java 17

➔ Sealed class/Interface enables more fine-grained inheritance control inJava.
➔ Sealing a class/Interface means we are defining the permitted subtypes.
➔ In simple words we can say that sealing a class or interface we are going to instruct ➔ those who will be able to extend it.somehow it's providing extra security to classes and interfaces. ➔ Only permitted subtypes will be able to extend sealed classes/interfaces.
➔ In java if you have a final class , it can not be extended, if it's abstract anyone can ➔ extend it BUT we had no control on who can extend the classes.

➔ In simple words we can say that class can either be extended or cant be BUT we can't restrict this extension to a given class.this is what sealed classes will do.
➔ Sealed classes/Interface can only be accessible for those whom you will permit,anyone else will NOT be able to extend it.
Sealed Interface

package palywithjava.in.sealedclasses; public sealed interface OrgService permits EmployeeService,TraineeService,SealedTester {

int getAge();
int getSalary();
}

From interface declaration, it's clear that Only EmployeeService(I),TraineeService(I) and SealedTester(C) will be able to extend the sealed Interface OrgService
Main purpose of using sealed classes/Interfaces is to provide modular security to our classes so that nobody will be able to access them until we are permitting them.

We need to follow some guidelines while using sealed classes.

### 1. Sealed classes/Interfaces can be extended within the package. 2. Sub classes/interfaces should implement/extend the sealed class/Interface. 3. Sub classes/interfaces should have either of the access modifier final,sealed and non-sealed.

final : it can't be extended.
sealed: only permitted type can extend.
non-sealed: this type will be available to be extended/implemented by any class/interface.

Code walkthrough

package palywithjava.in.sealedDemo;
public sealed interface OrgService permits EmployeeService,TraineeService { int getAge();
int getSalary();
}

If the subtype is a non-sealed interface then it will be extensible by everyone.

package palywithjava.in.sealedDemo;
public non-sealed interface EmployeeService extends OrgService {

String getEmployeeName();
}

If the subtype is a sealed interface then it will NOT be extensible by everyone. package palywithjava.in.sealedDemo;
public class EmployeeImpl implements EmployeeService{

@Override
public String getEmployeeName() {
return "Amit";

}
@Override
public int getAge() {

return 28;
}
@Override
public int getSalary() {

return 331110;
}

}
package palywithjava.in.sealedDemo;
public sealed interface TraineeService extends OrgService permits SealedTesterIntrface{

String getTraineeName();
}

package palywithjava.in.sealedDemo;
public non-sealed interface SealedTesterIntrface extends TraineeService {
}

package palywithjava.in.sealedDemo;
public class TraineeImpl implements SealedTesterIntrface{ @Override
public int getAge() {
return 25;
}
@Override
public int getSalary() {
return 333330;
}
@Override
public String getTraineeName() {
return "Raj";
}
}

![](image_rsrc3XB.jpg)Let's try to extend this interface from another Interface which is there in another package.
![](image_rsrc3XC.jpg)You can see the error “ Class is not allowed to extend sealed class from another package” You can use collections to get some info about Sealed Classes:

sealed class Product permits Laptop {
}
non-sealed class Laptop extends Product{ }

public class DemoTest {
public static void main(String[] args) {
Product p=new Product();
Product c = new Product();
Class cz = c.getClass();
Class<?>[] permittedSubclasses = cz.getPermittedSubclasses();
for (Class<?> sc : permittedSubclasses){

System.out.println(cz.isSealed()+" "+sc.getName());
}}} Result: true Laptop

### CompletableFuture in Java8

public class CompletableFuture<T> extends Object implements Future<T>,  CompletionStage<T>
●It enables us to write asynchronous code or non-blocking code in java

●It runs a task on a separate Thread from the main Thread and notifies the main thread about completion or failure.
●In this case, Main Thread need not block/wait for task completion and another task can run in parallel.
●Its a Future that can complete explicitly OR may be a CompletionStage where we can perform an additional action on completion/failure.
●It internally uses ForkJoinPool class to get the Executor instance to run jobs, ForkJoinPool is based on work-stealing algorithm where it will by default creates the same no of Thread as the available no of processors( this behavior can be customized if required) and Threads will automatically steal task for execution.

CompletableFuture is just an enhanced version of java.util.future.
//CompletableFuture with Runnable run() method with default Thread

CompletableFuture<Void> comp=CompletableFuture. runAsync(()->{ System.out.println("###### This is RUN Method");
System.out.println("##### Thread Name: "+Thread.currentThread().getName());

});
//CompletableFuture with Runnable run() method with Executor
Executor exec= Executors.newFixedThreadPool(5);
CompletableFuture<Void> comp1=CompletableFuture.runAsync(()->{

System. out.println("==> This is RUN Method");
System.out.println("==>Thread Name: "+Thread.currentThread().getName()); },exec);
System.out.println("||| Main Thread Name: "+Thread.currentThread().getName());

RESULT:
###### This is RUN Method
##### Thread Name: ForkJoinPool.commonPool-worker-1 ||| Main Thread Name: main
==> This is RUN Method
==>Thread Name: pool-1-thread-1

You can pass any Runnable instance as arg to runAsync(r) and you can even pass an Executor instance so that it will run on Executors instead of ForkJoinPool(default executor). Simplify the same code:
//Runnable(I) run method implementation

Runnable run=()->{
System.out.println("This is Runnable run method.");
System.out.println("##### Thread Name: "+Thread.currentThread().getName());

};
//CompletableFuture with Runnable run() method with default Thread CompletableFuture<Void> comp=CompletableFuture.runAsync(run); //CompletableFuture with Runnable run() method with Executor
Executor exec= Executors.newFixedThreadPool(5);
CompletableFuture<Void> comp1=CompletableFuture.runAsync(run,exec); System.out.println("||| Main Thread Name: "+Thread.currentThread().getName()); Output: This is Runnable run method.
##### Thread Name: ForkJoinPool.commonPool-worker-1
||| Main Thread Name: main
This is Runnable run method.
##### Thread Name: pool-1-thread-1

To Understand CompletableFuture, it's good to have a high-level walkthrough of the interfaces/methods.
Interface Future<I>
public interface Future<V>

●Future also enables non-blocking code with some limitations. ●It can't be completed manually
●We can't combine multiple Future objects and no proper exception handling

Interface CompletionStage<T>
public interface CompletionStage<T>

●A stage of a possibly asynchronous computation, that performs an action or computes a value when another CompletionStage completes.
●A stage used to be completed once it's terminated but it can also trigger another dependent job in between.
●Multiple completionStage can be chained together.
●Multiple jobs can be chained using Functional interfaces like Runnable, Callable, Function, and Consumer . We will look into Functional Interface and Stream API in detail in our next blog.

Example:
stage.thenApply(x -> square(x)).thenAccept(x -> System.out.print(x)).thenRun(() -> System.out.println())
Most commonly used methods in CompletableFuture
static

CompletableFuture <Void> runAsync(Runnable runnable)
Returns a new CompletableFuture that is asynchronously completed by a task running in the ForkJoinPool.commonPool() after it runs the given action.

static

CompletableFuture <Void> runAsync(Runnable runnable, Executor executor)
Returns a new CompletableFuture that is asynchronously completed by a task running in the given executor after it runs the given action.

static <U>

CompletableFuture <U> supplyAsync(Supplier<U> supplier)
Returns a new CompletableFuture that is asynchronously completed by a task running in the ForkJoinPool.commonPool() with the value obtained by calling the given Supplier.

static <U>

CompletableFuture <U> supplyAsync(Supplier<U> supplier, Executor executor) Returns a new CompletableFuture that is asynchronously completed by a task running in the given executor with the value obtained by calling the given Supplier.

Now we have to see that when we have to go for runAsync(Runnable r) and supplyAsync(Supplier<U> supplier).
runAsync(Runnable r) : when No Return type is expected
supplyAsync(Supplier<U> supplier): When result is expected.

Example : Sort all Employee data based on Enmployee_name in descending order. import playwithjava.in.util.EmplUtil;
import java.util.Comparator;
import java.util.concurrent.CompletableFuture;
public class Java8Demo {

public static void main(String[] args)throws Exception {
CompletableFuture<Void> completableFuture = CompletableFuture.runAsync(new

Runnable() {
@Override
public void run() {

// EmplUtil.getAllEmployeesData() will retun List of ALL Employee EmplUtil.getAllEmployeesData().stream()

.sorted(Comparator. comparing(EmployeeData::getName) .reversed())
.forEach(System.out::println);
}
});
completableFuture.get();
}
}

Using Lambda:

CompletableFuture<Void> completableFuture2 = CompletableFuture. runAsync(()-> { // EmplUtil.getAllEmployeesData() will retun List of ALL Employee EmplUtil.getAllEmployeesData().stream()

.sorted(Comparator. comparing(EmployeeData::getName)
.reversed())
.forEach(System.out::println);
});
completableFuture.get();

Use case 2: get Employee Data based on ascending order of salary.

import playwithjava.in.util.EmplUtil;
package playwithjava.in;
import playwithjava.in.bean.EmployeeData;
import playwithjava.in.util.EmplUtil;
import java.util.Comparator;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.concurrent.CompletableFuture;
import java.util.function.Supplier;
import java.util.stream.Collectors;
public class Java8Demo {

public static void main(String[] args)throws Exception {
//supplyAsync WILL take Supplier(Functional Interface) as input // We will look into most commonly used
CompletableFuture<List<EmployeeData>> completableFuture =

CompletableFuture. supplyAsync(new Supplier<List<EmployeeData>>() { @Override
public List<EmployeeData> get() {

return EmplUtil.getAllEmployeesData()
.parallelStream()
.sorted(Comparator.comparing(EmployeeData::getAnnualSalary)) .collect(Collectors.toList());

}
});
List<EmployeeData> result=completableFuture.get();
result.forEach(x-> System.out.println(x));

}
}
I have just taken a simple example to explain the use cases.
We have many methods in CompletableFuture to join tasks and do parallel jobs in async mode.
For more info please visit official docs if required or please do let me know so that i can cover all those methods. Using lambda:

import playwithjava.in.util.EmplUtil;
import java.util.Comparator;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.concurrent.CompletableFuture;
import java.util.stream.Collectors;
public class Java8Demo {

public static void main(String[] args)throws Exception {
//supplyAsync WILL take Supplier(Functional Interface) as input
// We will look into most commonly used Functional Interface in next blog CompletableFuture<List<EmployeeData>> completableFuture = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(()->EmplUtil.getAllEmployeesData() .parallelStream()
.sorted(Comparator.comparing(EmployeeData::getAnnualSalary)) .collect(Collectors.toList()));
List<EmployeeData> empData=completableFuture.get();
empData.forEach(System.out::println);
}
}

If you want to use your own Executorservice:

Executor executor= Executors.newFixedThreadPool(5);//your Executer CompletableFuture<List<EmployeeData>> completableFuture = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(()-> {

return EmplUtil.getAllEmployeesData()
.parallelStream()

.sorted(Comparator. comparing(EmployeeData::getAnnualSalary)) .collect(Collectors.toList());
},executor);
List<EmployeeData> result=completableFuture.get();
result.forEach(x-> System.out.println(x));

If you will use supplyAsync(..) and runAsync(..) without Executor Service then CompletableFuture will use ForkJoinPool to create no of Threads and that will be by default same as no of available processors.
Walkthrough of Other Important methods of CompletableFuture
CompletableFuture_Intance.thenXXX(Functional Interface Instance)
Example:
CompletableFuture_Instance.thenApply(Function_Instance)
Here Function is a Functional Interface that takes an input and returns another input. we can use it for data manipulation.
Add “Mr.” to the name of each Employee in async.

Code walkthrough CompletableFuture_Instance.thenApply(Function_Instance)

CompletableFuture<List<String>> currentWorkingEmpData=CompletableFuture .supplyAsync(()-> EmplUtil.getAllCurrentEmployeesData()
.stream()
.map(EmployeeData::getName)
.collect(Collectors.toList()));

CompletableFuture<List<String>> listCompletableFuture=currentWorkingEmpData .thenApply(x->x.stream()
.map(y -> "Mr. " + y)
.collect(Collectors.toList()));

List<String> names=listCompletableFuture.get();
System.out.println(names);
CompletableFuture_Instance.thenAccept(Consumer_Instance)

CompletableFuture<List<String>> currentWorkingEmpData=CompletableFuture .supplyAsync(()-> EmplUtil.getAllCurrentEmployeesData()
.stream()
.map(EmployeeData::getName)
.collect(Collectors.toList()));

currentWorkingEmpData.thenAccept(x->{
System.out.println(x);
}); //you can call any Consumer as part of thenAccept method arg
Just like the above example, we can use any Functional Interface method to execute the chain of CompletableFuture to execute in Async mode.
Combining 2 CompletableFuture results:
You can use any of the below methods as per requirements.

Methods :
thenCompose(Function<? super T,? extends CompletionStage<U>> fn)
Returns a new CompletionStage that, when this stage completes normally, is executed with this stage as the argument to the supplied function.

thenComposeAsync (Function<? super T,? extends CompletionStage<U>> fn)  Returns a new CompletionStage that, when this stage completes normally, is executed using this stage's default asynchronous execution facility, with this stage as the argument to the supplied function.

thenComposeAsync (Function<? super T,? extends CompletionStage<U>> fn, Executor executor)  Returns a new CompletionStage that, when this stage completes normally, is executed using the supplied Executor, with this stage's result as the argument to the supplied function.

CompletableFuture<List<EmployeeData>> listCompletableFuture2=listCompletableFuture1 .thenCompose(s->CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(()->Java8Demo.getOldEmpList(s)));
System.out.println(listCompletableFuture1.get());

Here both CompletableFuture returns the same type of object. If you want to combine 2 completely different Future.

thenCombine (CompletionStage<? extends U> other, BiFunction<? super T,? super U,? extends V> fn) Returns a new CompletionStage that, when this and the other given stage both complete normally, is executed with the two results as arguments to the supplied function.

thenCombineAsync (CompletionStage<? extends U> other, BiFunction<? super T,? super U,? extends V> fn)
Returns a new CompletionStage that, when this and the other given stage complete normally, is executed using this stage's default asynchronous execution facility, with the two results as arguments to the supplied function.

thenCombineAsync (CompletionStage<? extends U> other, BiFunction<? super T,? super U,? extends V> fn, Executor executor)
Returns a new CompletionStage that, when this and the other given stage complete normally, is executed using the supplied executor, with the two results as arguments to the supplied function.

CompletableFuture<String> completableFuture = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> "RAMA") .thenCombine(CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(

() -> " KRISHNA"), (s1, s2) -> s1 + s2)); Result: RAMA KRISHNA
Here 2nd arg is BiFunction, BiFunction is Functional interface when takes 2 input and returns output.
You can use Consumers if you don't want to return anything.

CompletableFuture<String> completableFuture
= CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> "RAMA") .thenCombine(CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(

() -> " KRISHNA"), (s1, s2) ->System.out.println(s1+s2))); Result: RAMA KRISHNA ,same result..but no return value.we are directly Printing using BiConsumer functional interface. Executing multiple Futures together:

CompletableFuture<String> completableFuture1
= CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> "RAM");
CompletableFuture<String> completableFuture2
= CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> "SYAM");
CompletableFuture<Void>
finalResult=CompletableFuture.allOf(completableFuture1,completableFuture2); System.out.println(finalResult.get());//NULL ,because finalResult is not combined result //We need to get it manually as below
System.out.println(completableFuture1.get()); //RAM
System.out.println(completableFuture2.get()); //SYAM

But if your requirements to get joined result then, String result= Stream.of(completableFuture1,completableFuture2) .map(CompletableFuture::join)
.collect(Collectors.joining(" "));

System.out.println(result); //RAM SYAM

## Lombok library in java

Let's look into some out of box features that we can use in java code while working on real time coding.

These features really help you to improve your skill.
Lombok is highly recommended to use real time projects in industry.you can use it to generate getter ,setter,constructors and many more.

Lombok is a Java library that provides annotations that help developers reduce boilerplate code. Lombok annotations can generate code such as getters and setters, constructors, and logging statements.

Some common Lombok Annotations: @Setter
@Getter
@Data
@NoArgsConstructor
@AllArgsConstructor
@ToString
@EqualsAndHashCode
@Slf4j
@Builder(toBuilder = true)

Add below maven dependency to your project class path(pom.xml)  @Getter :  it is used to create a getter method. It can be used at class level as well as at attribute level.
Without Lombok:With Lombok:

public class EmployeeRequest { String empName;
String empEmail;
Long empId;
public String getEmpName() {

return empName;
}
public String getEmpEmail() { return empEmail;
}
public Long getEmpId() { return empId;}}

import lombok.Getter;
@Getter
public class EmployeeRequest {

String empName;
String empEmail;
Long empId;

}
At Attribute Level:  public class EmployeeRequest {

@Getter
String empName; @Getter
String empEmail; @Getter
Long empId;

}
@Setter : it will create a setter method for class attributes.its applicable at class level as well as attribute level.
Without Lombok:  public class EmployeeRequest {

String empName;
String empEmail;
Long empId;
public void setEmpName(String empName) {

this .empName = empName;
}
public void setEmpEmail(String empEmail) {

this .empEmail = empEmail;
}
public void setEmpId(Long empId) {

this.empId = empId;
}
}
With Lombok:

import lombok.Getter;
@Setter
public class EmployeeRequest {

String empName;
String empEmail;
Long empId;

}
With getter and Setter Together:

import lombok.Getter;
import lombok.Setter;
@Getter
@Setter
public class EmployeeRequest {

String empName;
String empEmail;
Long empId;

}
It will create a getter() and setter(..) method for all the class attributes.
If you want to customize the behavior ,like you want to create a getter/setter for a given attribute only THEN apply these annotations at field level.
@Data: this annotation is a combination of @Getter and @Setter BUT it is applicable at class level ONLY.

import lombok.Data;
@Data
public class EmployeeRequest {

String empName;
String empEmail;
Long empId;

}
@NoArgsConstructor : it will create a no-argument constructor for the given class.

import lombok.Data;
import lombok.NoArgsConstructor; @NoArgsConstructor
public class EmployeeRequest {

String empName;
String empEmail;
Long empId;

} Above code is similar to below:

public class EmployeeRequest { String empName;
String empEmail;
Long empId;
public EmployeeRequest() { }

}
@AllArgsConstructor : it will create an all-arguments constructor for the given class.

@Data
@AllArgsConstructor
public class EmployeeRequest {

String empName;
String empEmail;
Long empId;

}
If you need to have no-arg constructor as well as all-args constructor.then use both annotations on required classes.

@Data
@NoArgsConstructor
@AllArgsConstructor
public class EmployeeRequest {

String empName;
String empEmail;
Long empId;

}
@EqualsAndHashCode: it will generate squalls() and hashCode(..) methods for the given class.
With Lombok:

@Data
@EqualsAndHashCode
public class EmployeeRequest {

String empName;
String empEmail;
Long empId;

}
Without Lombok:

@Data
public class EmployeeRequest {
String empName;
String empEmail;
Long empId;
@Override
public boolean equals(Object o) {
if (this == o) return true;
if (o == null || getClass() != o.getClass()) return false;
EmployeeRequest that = (EmployeeRequest) o;
return empName.equals(that.empName) && empEmail.equals(that.empEmail) && empId.equals(that.empId);
}
@Override
public int hashCode() {
return Objects.hash(empName, empEmail, empId);
}
}

@ToString : This will create a toString() method for the given class.  Without Lombok:

public class EmployeeRequest {
String empName;
String empEmail;
Long empId;
@Override
public String toString() {

return  "EmployeeRequest{" +
"empName='" + empName + '\'' + ", empEmail='" + empEmail + '\'' + ", empId=" + empId +
'}';

}
}
With Lombok:

@ToString
public class EmployeeRequest { String empName;
String empEmail;
Long empId;
}

@Builder :

it will create a mutable instance of the annotated class. It will be applicable at class level as well as at method level. This annotation can be applied at :
I. Class Level
Ii. Method Level
iii. Constructor level

@Builder annotation uses Builder Pattern and helps us to create immutable instances of annotated class. If we apply at class level , by default it will consider all the fields for creating Objects.
I. Class Level

@Builder (toBuilder = true) public class EmployeeRequest { String empName;
String empEmail;
Long empId;
}

Create Immutable instance of the class:  public class LombokDemoClass {
public static void main(String[] args) {

EmployeeRequest employeeRequest=EmployeeRequest. builder() .empId(110l)
.empName("Raj")
.empEmail("raj@gmail.com")
.build();

}
}
Constructor level:

public class EmployeeRequest {
String empName;
String empEmail;
Long empId;
@Builder(toBuilder = true)
public EmployeeRequest(String empName, String empEmail) {

this.empName = empName;
this.empEmail = empEmail;
}
}
Create Immutable instance of the class:  public class LombokDemoClass {
public static void main(String[] args) {

EmployeeRequest employeeRequest=EmployeeRequest. builder() .empName("Raj")
.empEmail("raj@gmail.com")
.empId(110l) //This Attribute will NOT be considered .build();

}
}
Method Level:

@Data
public class EmployeeRequest {
String empName;
String empEmail;
Long empId;
@Builder(toBuilder = true)
public EmployeeRequest getClassInstance(String empName, String empEmail) { EmployeeRequest employeeRequest=new EmployeeRequest(); employeeRequest.setEmpName("Raj");
employeeRequest.setEmpEmail("Raj@gmail");
return employeeRequest;
}
}

@slf4j: This annotation generates a logger instance with thedefault name ‘log’.
@Slf4j
public class LombokDemoClass { public static void main(String[] args) { log.info("########### Test ....."); }
}
@Synchronized:
This annotation is used on methods to make Java methods synchronized. This is for thread safety.

@Synchronized
public String getName(){
System.out.println("====== test ======");
return "Raj";
}

@NonNull:

This annotation on the parameter of a constructor or a method generates a null check. If put on a parameter, lombok will insert a null-check at the start of the method / constructor's body, throwing a NullPointerException with the parameter's name as a message. If put on a field, any generated method assigning a value to this field will also produce these null-checks.

@NonNull
@Builder(toBuilder = true)
public EmployeeRequest getClassInstance(String empName, String empEmail) {

EmployeeRequest employeeRequest= new EmployeeRequest();
employeeRequest.setEmpName(empName);
employeeRequest.setEmpEmail(empEmail);
return employeeRequest;

}

## What is new in Java 21

### Virtual Thread
Being a Java Developer , You must know what is Thread?

A Thread is a light-weighted process, or we can say the smallest part of the process that allows a program to execute that task.
Threads can be used to perform complicated tasks in the background without interrupting the main program.
Let's assume , I have to print “Welcome” 1 times, I will simply create a Thread and Thread will take care of printing the String “Welcome” 1 times as required.
In Java, We can create a Thread either by extending Thread class or by implementing Runnable Interface.
Hope you already know this. Create Thread by Extending Thread class: public class MyThreadDemo extends Thread{  public void run() {
System.out.println("Welcome"); }  public static void main(String[] args) {
MyThreadDemo th=new MyThreadDemo(); th.start();
}
}  Result: This code is running in a thread Create Thread with Runnable:
public class ThreadWithRunnableDemo implements Runnable {  @Override  public void run() {
System.out.println("Welcome"); }  public static void main(String[] args) {  // Create Object of ThreadWithRunnableDemo  ThreadWithRunnableDemo threadWithRunnableDemo=new ThreadWithRunnableDemo();  //Create Thread  Thread th=new Thread(threadWithRunnableDemo);  //Start Thread th.start(); }
}  Result: Welcome
We can simplify the process of Thread creation using Lambda expression: public class ThreadWithLambdaDemo { public static void main(String[] args) { //implement run() method of Runnable Runnable th=()->System.out.println("Welcome"); //start Thread  new Thread(th).start();

//OR
Thread thread=new Thread(()-> System.out.println("Welcome")); thread.start();

}}
Result: Welcome

### More about the Virtual Thread

As of Now we have seen that , We can create a Thread either by extending Thread class OR by implementing Runnable interface and we can even use Lambda Expression to simplify the code.

But our intention is NOT to just create a Thread ,creating a Thread in Java consumes some resources and memory from the system.

### Thread can either of below type:

●OS Thread
●Platform Thread
●Virtual Threads

java.lang.Thread , Thread created using Thread class or Runnable interface are Platform Thread.
These Platform threads are typically mapped 1:1 to kernel threads scheduled by the OS.
OS threads are quite “heavy”. That makes them suitable for executing all types of tasks.
Depending on the OS and configuration, they consume somewhere between 2 and 10 MB by default.

Normally making requests to the server, each request consumes a Thread,So if you want to utilize a million requests/threads in your heavy-load concurrent application, you better have more than 2 TB of memory to spare.

As you can see that, creating too many platform Thread is NOT the better option since it will consume huge resources and slow down the system because OS Threads are limited, any system will NOT allow you to create more that limited Platform Threads because each Platform Thread is mapped with OS Thread.

So creating more Platform Thread than a limited number is just like waiting for OS Thread to be available so that Platform Thread can be mapped with OS Thread.

In Java, We used to have a Thread pool and we can simply pool Threads from the pool based on our requirements BUT as we know this has also a limitation on how many Platform Threads can be created.

What will happen if you create more Platform Threads than the allowed limit ? In simple words we can say that it will create Performance issues,deadlock,starvation, memory issues or even a system can be crashed.
Suppose an application with an average latency of 50ms achieves a throughput of 200 requests per second by processing 10 requests concurrently.

In order for that application to scale to a throughput of 2000 requests per second, it will need to process 100 requests concurrently. If each request is handled in a thread for the request's duration then, for the application to keep up and working , the number of threads must grow as throughput grows.

Unfortunately, the number of available threads is limited because the JDK implements threads as wrappers around operating system (OS) threads. OS threads are costly, so we cannot have too many of them, which makes the implementation ill-suited to the thread-per-request style.

If each request consumes a thread, and thus an OS thread, for its duration, then the number of threads often becomes the limiting factor long before other resources, such as CPU or network connections, are exhausted.

The JDK's current implementation of threads caps the application's throughput to a level well below what the hardware can support. This happens even when threads are pooled, since pooling helps avoid the high cost of starting a new thread but does not increase the total number of threads.

## => THis is where Virtual Thread comes into picture.

=>Here we will create Virtual Threads instead of Platform Thread.

Now The question is , how Virtual Threads and Platform Threads differ. Suppose a System has capacity to handle 500 Threads for the given throughput and given duration, what if you need 700 Threads to handle the in-coming requests and expect the same throughput at the same time ? In such case operating systems give the illusion of plentiful memory by mapping a large virtual address space to a limited amount of physical RAM, a Java runtime can give the illusion of plentiful threads by mapping a large number of virtual threads to a small number of OS threads.

### Virtual Thread and Platform Threads are instances of java.lang.Thread

Virtual Thread not tied to a particular OS thread directly.
A platform thread is implemented in the traditional way, as a thin wrapper around an OS thread.
Virtual Thread is cheap and lightweight so JVM creates it instead of pooling it from Thread pool.
Virtual threads are a lightweight implementation of threads that is provided by the JDK rather than the OS.

Virtual threads employ M:N scheduling, where a large number (M) of virtual threads is scheduled to run on a smaller number (N) of OS threads.

![](image_rsrc3XD.jpg)

Today, every instance of java.lang.Thread in the JDK is a platform thread. A platform thread runs Java code on an underlying OS thread and captures the OS thread for the code's entire lifetime. The number of platform threads is limited to the number of OS threads.

A virtual thread is also an instance of java.lang.Thread that runs Java code on an underlying OS thread but does not capture the OS thread for the code's entire lifetime. This means that many virtual threads can run their Java code on the same OS thread.

While a platform thread monopolizes a precious OS thread, a virtual thread does not.
Since Virtual Threads are lightweight, the number of virtual threads can be much larger than the number of OS threads for the same system configuration.

we can create a very high number of virtual threads ( millions) in an application without depending on the number of platform threads. These virtual threads are managed by JVM , lightweight in nature and stored in RAM as normal Java objects.

Key points:
1\. Virtual Threads are always demon threads in nature.
2\. Virtual Thread always has medium priority and it can't be changed.
3\. Virtual Threads are lightweight and short lived.
4\. Virtual Threads are NOT active members of Thread groups.
5\. Virtual threads do not support the stop(), suspend(), or resume() methods.
6\. Virtual Threads are NOT directly connected with OS Thread.
7\. One Platform Thread can be mapped with many Virtual Threads.
8\. Virtual threads are suitable for executing tasks that spend most of the time blocked, often waiting for I/O operations to complete.
9\. Virtual threads are not intended for long running CPU intensive operations.

There are multiple ways to create Virtual Thread,we can create usingThread class OR Thread.Builder.

}

}

### Compare Virtual Threads and Platform Threads

import java.time.Duration;
import java.time.Instant;
import java.util.concurrent.Executors;
import java.util.concurrent.atomic.AtomicInteger;

public class VirtuaThANdPlatformThDemo { public static void main(String[] args) { final AtomicInteger atomicInteger = new AtomicInteger(); Runnable runnable = () -> { try {

Thread. sleep(Duration.ofSeconds(1));
} catch(Exception e) {
System.out.println(e);
}
System.out.println("Welcome -"+ atomicInteger.incrementAndGet()); };

Instant start = Instant.now(); try (var executor = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(100)) {

for (int i = 0; i < 10_000; i++) {
executor.submit(runnable);
}
}

Instant finish = Instant. now();
long timeElapsed = Duration.between(start, finish).toMillis(); System.out.println("Total Elapsed Time : " + timeElapsed);

}
}
Result:

Welcome - 1
Welcome - 2
Welcome - 3
.
.
.
Welcome - 9999
Welcome - 9990
Total Elapsed Time : 100300 ms [//Approx 100 seconds]

### Explanation:

As we have created 10,000 threads from this Runnable and executed them with virtual threads and platform threads to compare the performance of both. We will use the Duration.between() api to measure the total time in executing all the tasks.

First, we are using a pool of 100 platform threads. In this way, Executor will be able to run 100 tasks at a time and other tasks will need to wait. As we have 10,000 tasks, the total time to finish the execution will be approximately 100300 ms i.e 100 seconds.

### Now we will create Virtual Threads so replace the Executors.newFixedThreadPool(100) with Executors.newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor(). This will execute all the tasks in virtual threads instead of platform threads.

Now you will be able to see the difference between Virtual Threads and Platform Threads.
As we have to wait for each call , for such use-cases it is recommended to use Virtual Threads instead of Platform Threads.

}
System.out.println("Welcome - " +atomicInteger.incrementAndGet()); };

Instant start = Instant.now(); try (var executor=Executors.newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor()) { for(int i = 0; i < 10_000; i++) {
executor.submit(runnable);
}
}

Instant finish = Instant. now();
long timeElapsed = Duration.between(start, finish).toMillis(); System.out.println("Total elapsed time : " + timeElapsed);

}
}

Welcome - 3
.
.

Welcome - 10000

As you can see in the above code, it's just the difference of creating Virtual Thread OR Platform Thread.
var executor = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(100); var executor = Executors.newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor();
Explanation:

As you can see the total time taken to execute 10,000 times, Platform Thread is taking 100 seconds while Virtual Threads take on 1.5 seconds. It's around 70 times faster with the same throughput.

NOTE - As you can see the above example, Virtual Threads executed the job 70 times faster than the Platform Threads BUT it doesn't mean that Virtual Threads are FASTER than Platform Thread.

Virtual Threads are just the way to get higher throughput on the same system configuration at the same time. Let's increase the Virtual Thread count to 700,000 instead of 10,000 and see how much time it's taking on the same system.

As you can see in the example below, 700,000 virtual threads are taking just 10 seconds to execute all the jobs.

the main thread is not blocked when a given task is executed by a virtual thread.

join(Duration duration) //wait in a time-bound manner join(long millis)

### Deep dive:

Developers can choose whether to use virtual threads or platform threads. Here is an example program that creates a large number of virtual threads. The program first obtains anExecutorService that will create a new virtual thread for each submitted task.

It then submits 10,000 tasks and waits for all of them to complete:
try (var executor = Executors.newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor()) {

IntStream.range(0, 10_000).forEach(i -> {
executor.submit(() -> {
Thread.sleep(Duration.ofSeconds(1));
return i;

});
});
}

The task in this example is simple code — sleep for one second — and modern hardware can easily support 10,000 virtual threads running such code concurrently. Behind the scenes, the JDK runs the code on a small number of OS threads, perhaps as few as one.

We are using an ExecutorService that obtains platform threads from a pool, such as Executors.newFixedThreadPool(200). TheExecutorService would create 200 platform threads to be shared by all 10,000 tasks, so many of the tasks would run sequentially rather than concurrently and the program would take a long time to complete.

For this program, a pool with 200 platform threads can only achieve a throughput of 200 tasks-per-second, whereas virtual threads achieve a throughput of about 10,000 tasks-persecond (after sufficient warmup).

As we have changed the Thread count to 700,000 so the program will submit 700,000 tasks and all will run concurrently.Before using Virtual Threads, We should be knowing where to use Virtual Threads.
Suppose your task is to sort an array and its taking 1sec so it doesn't matter whether its Platform Thread OR Virtual Threads.Virtual Threads are mostly applicable to use where there is waiting/sleep kind of task is involved.

Virtual threads are not faster threads— they do not run code any faster than platform threads. They exist to provide scale (higher throughput), not speed (lower latency). Virtual Threads are mainly useful when :
■ The number of concurrent tasks is high
■ The workload is not CPU-bound, since having many more threads than processor cores cannot improve throughput in that case.

### Virtual Threads with Web Application/Microservices

As we have gone through, how does Virtual Thread work ? Let's assume we have a Web Application that receives around millions of requests per second to fetch some data from remote services.if we prefer to opt thread-per-request model than we need around 1 million threads to process all the request, creating 1 million Threads in any application is costly process so its recommended to create Virtual Threads to process the request in this case and many WEB framework are evening supporting it.

our code will look something like below.
![](image_rsrc3XF.jpg)Java structured concurrency provides multiple options to take care of such use cases.

### How to observe Virtual Threads

Just writing a clean executable code for multithreaded environments is not enough ,we should also think about troubleshooting, maintenance, and optimization.
Java does provide various tools and mechanisms to track Treads, Java debugger needed to get Thread call stacks, and inspect variables in stack frames.

JDK Flight Recorder (JFR), which is the JDK's low-overhead profiling and monitoring mechanism, can associate events from application code with the correct virtual thread. These tools cannot do these things for applications written in the asynchronous style. In that style tasks are not related to threads, so debuggers cannot display or manipulate the state of a task, and profilers cannot tell how much time a task spends waiting for I/O.

Traditional Java Thread dump is another option for troubleshooting in case of Threadper-request BUT its not enough in case of Virtual Threads because we can have millions of threads running concurrently.

### Java has introduced a new kind of thread dump injcmd to present virtual threads

alongside platform threads, all grouped in a meaningful way.
JSON format of thread dump can be get using below command: $ jcmd <pid> Thread.dump_to_file -format=json <file>

### How does Virtual Thread works internally

Any thread executes some task once it's scheduled for execution on a processor core, in case of Platform Thread which is a wrapper of OS Thread, JDK relies on the scheduler in the OS which used to take care of Thread scheduling.

In-case of Virtual Thread,JDK has its own scheduler. Instead of assigning virtual threads to processors directly, the JDK's scheduler assigns virtual threads to platform threads in M:N ration ,where multiple Virtual Threads can be assigned to a single Platform Thread.

The JDK's virtual thread scheduler is a work-stealing ForkJoinPool that operates in FIFO mode. The parallelism of the scheduler is the number of platform threads available for the purpose of scheduling virtual threads. By default it is equal to the number of available processors, but it can be tuned with the system property

### jdk.virtualThreadScheduler.parallelism.

To run code in a virtual thread, the JDK's virtual thread scheduler assigns the virtual thread for execution on a platform thread by mounting the virtual thread on a platform thread. This makes the platform thread become the carrier of the virtual thread. Later, after running some code, the virtual thread can unmount from its carrier. At that point the platform thread is free so the scheduler can mount a different virtual thread on it, thereby making it a carrier again.

![](image_rsrc3XG.jpg)As you can see here,JVM Scheduler schedules Platform Threads with Virtual Threads and OS Scheduler takes care of scheduling OS Thread with Platform Thread

## Sequenced Collections

Before moving forward for Sequenced Collection ,Lets understand collection and its class/interface hierarchy as per java 21 API docs.

### The root interface in the collection hierarchy is Collection Interface.

A collection represents a group of objects. Some collections allow duplicate elements and others do not. Some are ordered, and others are unordered.
Some new interfaces have been introduced as part of java 21 that allow the existing collection classes to have a defined encounter order.
The order will have a well-defined first element, the second element, and so forth, up to the last element. These new interfaces are:

● SequencedCollection
● SequencedSet
● SequencedMap

Collections that have a defined encounter order are generally subtypes of the SequencedCollection interface.

### Why do we need SequencedCollection ?

Before Java 21, Java collections classes had no handy method to get first,last element,or adding element at first and last position as well as getting the reversed order of collection using predefined methods.

Being a developer , we used to do all this stuff manually as per our requirements but now as part of Java 21,this issue has been resolved.
Sequenced Collection is A collection that has a well-defined encounter order and supports operations at both ends, and that is reversible.
SequencedCollection is an Interface introduced as part of Java 21, similar to that SequencedMap and SequencedSet were introduced.

### SequencedMap

Like SequencedSet, SequencedMap contains the characteristics of SequencedCollection and Map.
![](image_rsrc3XN.jpg)

● A SequencedMap provides methods to add, retrieve and remove elements at either end of the map.
● This interface also defines the reversed() method, which provides a reverse-ordered view of this map.
● A SequencedMap has a well-defined encounter order that supports operations at both ends and is reversible.
● A map's reverse-ordered view is generally not serializable, even if the original map is serializable.
● The encounter order of a SequencedMap is similar to that of the elements of a SequencedCollection, but the ordering applies to mappings instead of individual elements.

Interface Declaration as per Java 21
![](image_rsrc3XP.jpg)Method Details:
![](image_rsrc3XR.jpg)SequencedMap Demo:

System.out.println("Add Element at 1st and last potion ::"); data.putFirst(5,"Raju");
data.putLast(9,"Rajesh");
System.out.println("Map Data after Adding elements : "+data);

SequencedMap<Integer,String> reversedMap=data.reversed(); System.out.println("Reversed Map : "+reversedMap); }
}
